# Categories with conjugation and octagons of Witt groups

**arXiv ID**: 2607.06454v2
**Authors**: David W. Lewis, Jean-Pierre Tignol
**Published**: 2026-07-07
**Categories**: math.KT, math.CT
**Comments**: The first author is deceased
**DOI**: 10.1016/j.jalgebra.2026.06.024
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.06454v2

## Abstract

A categorical approach is proposed for the construction of 8-periodic chain complexes of Witt groups of categories with duality. Exactness of the resulting sequences is proved under the hypothesis that the categories are abelian and artinian or when the endomorphism algebra of each object is semilocal and rad-adically complete.

## Full Text

Categories with conjugation and octagons of Witt groups

## Categories with conjugation
and
octagons of Witt groupsDavid W. Lewis†and Jean-Pierre TignolUCLouvain, ICTEAM Institute
B-1348 Louvain-la-Neuve, Belgiumjean-pierre.tignol@uclouvain.be

## Abstract.

A categorical approach is proposed for the construction of88-periodic chain complexes of Witt groups of categories with
duality. Exactness of the
resulting sequences is proved under the hypothesis that
the categories are abelian and artinian, or when the endomorphism
algebra of each object is semilocal and rad-adically complete.

## Key words and phrases:Category with duality, hermitian form

## Introduction

In the algebraic theory of quadratic forms, the special rôle played
by
quadratic field extensions was noticed early. Among the first
observations stands out the description by
Pfister[19, p. 123]of quadratic forms that become
hyperbolic over a given quadratic extension, reformulated in terms of
hermitian forms by Milnor–Husemoller: see[17, App. 2], where it
appears as a44-term exact sequence. Using Scharlau’s transfer,
Elman–Lam prolonged the sequence into a long33-periodic exact
sequence (an “exact triangle” or “symmetric exact hexagon”,
see[5, Th. 2.6]).
Extending this
early result in the framework of hermitian or skew-hermitian forms
over quaternion algebras, the first author uncovered a more complex
behaviour, giving rise to longer exact sequences relating forms over
quaternion algebras and over quadratic subfields, see[13],[14](see also[21, Th. 10.3.2]). It was soon realised that exact
sequences thus
constructed are88-periodic, and can therefore be folded into exact
octagons,
see[15]. Meanwhile, Ranicki also constructed88-periodic exact
sequences of Witt groups for “twisted quadratic extensions” viaLL-theory: see[20]and[8, (0.2)].

Building on the first author’s work,
Parimala–Sridharan–Suresh[4, Appendix 2]extended
the scope of[14]beyond the case of quaternion algebras,
obtaining exact sequences relating Witt groups of hermitian or
skew-hermitian forms over a central simple algebra and over the
centraliser of a quadratic subfield. These exact sequences were
further patched into exact octagons of Witt groups by
Grenier-Boley–Mahmoudi[7]. The latest development in this
line of investigation is due to First[6], who obtained an
analogous exact octagon of Witt groups for Azumaya algebras over
semilocal rings. His paper also discusses several important
applications.

The purpose of this work is to describe a categorical mechanism that
produces the octagons referred to above. Our approach is close in
spirit to the method in[16]making use of the periodicity of the
Clifford algebra construction and Morita equivalences. The basic tool
is a notion ofconjugationon an additive pseudo-abelian (=
idempotent-complete) category with12\frac{1}{2}(i.e., such that22is
invertible in eachHom\operatorname{Hom}group). A conjugation is a covariant version
of a duality,
consisting of an endofunctorι\iotaand a natural isomorphismλ:ι​ι⇒∼Id\lambda\colon\iota\iota\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\operatorname{Id}. The construction of the
hermitian category of symmetric spaces from a category with duality
has an analogue for a category with conjugation(𝒞,ι,λ)(\mathcal{C},\iota,\lambda), which yields adoublecategory with
conjugation(𝒞′,ι′,λ′)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}). The categories𝒞\mathcal{C}and𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}are related by a pair of functorsF:𝒞→𝒞′F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}andG:𝒞′→𝒞G\colon\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}. The functorFFis reminiscent of the
construction of hyperbolic symmetric spaces in the context of
categories with duality, whileGGis a forgetful functor. Iterating
the doubling construction yields an infinite sequence of categories
with conjugation(𝒞′′,ι′′,λ′′)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime}),(𝒞′′′,ι′′′,λ′′′)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime\prime}), … and functors𝒞\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}F\scriptstyle{F}𝒞′\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}F′\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}}𝒞′′\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}F′′\scriptstyle{F^{\prime\prime}}⋯\textstyle{\cdots}and𝒞\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}𝒞′\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}G\scriptstyle{G}𝒞′′\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}G′\scriptstyle{G^{\prime}}⋯\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}G′′\scriptstyle{G^{\prime\prime}}

The corresponding hermitian categories of symmetric spaces discussed
in §3are
similarly organised, see (3.2), and we show in
Theorem3.1that under the composition of two
consecutive functors from the88-periodic sequence the image of each
hermitian category consists of hyperbolic symmetric spaces.

Defining a Witt group from the hermitian category of a category with
duality(𝒞,D,δ)(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)requires an exact structure on𝒞\mathcal{C},
which provides the definition of metabolic symmetric spaces,
see[2, §1.2.4]. We consider two special cases
in §4. For thesplit exact structure, in
which exact sequences split, metabolic symmetric spaces are
hyperbolic, and Theorem3.1readily yields
from (0.1) an88-periodic chain complex. Exactness
requires a kind of Witt cancellation property (see
Remark4.1), which we establish under the hypothesis that
the endomorphism algebra of every object in𝒞\mathcal{C}is semilocal and
rad-adically complete: see Theorem4.6. This theorem doesnotcover the case discussed by First in[6], which
deals with
the split exact category of finitely generated projective modules over
Azumaya algebras over semilocal rings, but it yields the
Grenier-Boley–Mahmoudi octagon[7]as a special case.

When𝒞\mathcal{C}is an abelian category, with its usual exact
structure, our main result establishes
the exactness of the
octagon of Witt groups derived from (0.1) under the
hypothesis that the abelian category𝒞\mathcal{C}is artinian: see
Theorem4.8.
This result is sufficient to account for the exact octagon
from[15], and also for Warshauer’s exact octagon
of Witt groups of asymmetric spaces in[25]. By contrast, since
characteristic22is ruled out from the outset in this work, our
results do not cover the version of[14]in
characteristic22given by Knus–Villa in[10, Th. 3.8].

The sequence of categories with duality(𝒞,D,δ)(\mathcal{C},D,\delta),(𝒞,ι​D,−δα)(\mathcal{C},\iota D,-\delta_{\alpha}),(𝒞,D,−δ)(\mathcal{C},D,-\delta),(𝒞,ι​D,δα)(\mathcal{C},\iota D,\delta_{\alpha})extracted from the88-periodic
sequence (0.1) bears some formal resemblance with the
sequence of shifted dualitiesTn​(𝒞,D,δ)T^{n}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)of a triangulated
category with duality, which also satisfiesTn+2​(𝒞,D,δ)≡Tn​(𝒞,D,−δ)T^{n+2}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)\equiv T^{n}(\mathcal{C},D,-\delta)for alln∈ℤn\in\mathbb{Z}and is
therefore44-periodic, see[2, 63–65]. However, a triangulation on
an additive category is a very different feature than a conjugation,
as witnessed by the category𝒫A\mathcal{P}_{A}of finitely generated right
modules over an artinian simple ringAAwith involution, with the
usual duality(D,δ)(D,\delta)defined in §3.3below
and the split exact structure: it is easy to find examples of
conjugations commuting with(D,δ)(D,\delta)such that all the Witt groups
obtained from the sequence(𝒫A,D,δ)(\mathcal{P}_{A},D,\delta),(𝒫A,ι​D,−δα)(\mathcal{P}_{A},\iota D,-\delta_{\alpha}),(𝒫A,D,−δ)(\mathcal{P}_{A},D,-\delta),(𝒫A,ι​D,δα)(\mathcal{P}_{A},\iota D,\delta_{\alpha})(which are Witt groups of hermitian spaces) are
nonzero, whereas the odd-indexed derived Witt groups vanish,
see[1, Th. 4.2].

The examples of octagons worked out in detail in this work are all
based on categories of modules, but even in this particular case they
do not exhaust the full range of possibilities (see
Remark1.3). The formalism of conjugations extends
much farther; it will hopefully be useful in geometric settings for
more general exact categories with duality.

## 1.Categories with conjugation

Throughout this section,𝒞\mathcal{C}denotes akk-linear category for an
arbitrary commutative ringkk, and all the functors are tacitly
assumed to bekk-linear. Although this is not necessary for the
very first definitions, we assume22is invertible inkkand𝒞\mathcal{C}is pseudo-abelian (=idempotent-complete, see[9, Ch. I, Prop. 6.9]), which means that every
idempotent morphism splits. The latter
property holds for instance for the category of finitely-generated
projective modules over an arbitrarykk-algebra.

Using theι\iota-fixed unitλ∈A\lambda\in Ain the definition ofA′A^{\prime}, we define a natural isomorphismλ:ι​ι⇒∼IdℳA\lambda\colon\,\iota\iota\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{M}_{A}}as follows:λM:ι​ι​(M)→M,xι​ι↦x​λfor everyM∈ℳAand everyx∈M.\lambda_{M}\colon\,\iota\iota(M)\to M,\quad{}^{\iota\iota}x\mapsto x\lambda\qquad\text{for every $M\in\mathcal{M}_{A}$ and every $x\in M$}.

## Remark 1.3.

The example discussed in this subsection is not the most general
conjugation on a category of modules: in a general conjugation(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)onℳA\mathcal{M}_{A}the functorι\iotais an
auto-equivalence ofℳA\mathcal{M}_{A}, hence Morita theory shows that, up to
isomorphism,ι\iotais given byι​(M)=M⊗APfor some invertibleA-A-bimoduleP.\iota(M)=M\otimes_{A}P\qquad\text{for some invertible
$A$-$A$-bimodule $P$.}

(See[3, Ch. II, §5].) The isomorphismλ\lambdais given
byλM:M⊗AP⊗AP→M,m⊗p⊗q↦m​β​(p⊗q)\lambda_{M}\colon M\otimes_{A}P\otimes_{A}P\to M,\qquad m\otimes p\otimes q\mapsto m\,\beta(p\otimes q)

for some isomorphism of bimodulesβ:P⊗AP→A\beta\colon P\otimes_{A}P\to Asatisfying the associativity conditionβ​(p1⊗p2)⊗p3=p1​β​(p2⊗p3)\beta(p_{1}\otimes p_{2})\otimes p_{3}=p_{1}\beta(p_{2}\otimes p_{3})forp1p_{1},p2p_{2},p3∈Pp_{3}\in P. According to[3, Prop. 5.2, p. 73], the example
discussed above is the special case wherePPis isomorphic toAAas a leftAA-module. It then yields the automorphism ofAAdenoted
(abusively) byι\iotaabove because, up to isomorphism,P={xι1∣x∈A}P=\{{}_{1}x_{\iota}\mid x\in A\}wherea⋅xι1=a1​xιa\cdot{}_{1}x_{\iota}={}_{1}ax_{\iota}andxι1⋅a=x1​ι​(a)ι{}_{1}x_{\iota}\cdot a={}_{1}x\iota(a)_{\iota}forxx,a∈Aa\in A. The mapβ:P⊗AP→A\beta\colon P\otimes_{A}P\to Ais then given
byβ​(xι1⊗yι1)=x​ι​(y)​λ\beta({}_{1}x_{\iota}\otimes{}_{1}y_{\iota})=x\iota(y)\lambdawhereλ∈A×\lambda\in A^{\times}is subject toι​(λ)=λ\iota(\lambda)=\lambdaandι2​(a)=λ​a​λ−1\iota^{2}(a)=\lambda a\lambda^{-1}for alla∈Aa\in A.

WhenAAis commutative, the pairs(P,β)(P,\beta)that yield a
conjugation onℳA\mathcal{M}_{A}with the same action ofAAonPPon the
left and on the right are exactly the discriminant modules discussed
in[11, Ch. III, §3]. Since22is invertible inAA, these
discriminant modules are in one-to-one correspondence with the
separable quadraticAA-algebras,
see[11, Prop. III(4.2.4)]. Therefore, every
separable quadraticAA-algebra arises asA′A^{\prime}for a suitable
conjugation(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)onℳA\mathcal{M}_{A}.

## 2.Conjugations and dualities

Recall from[2, §1.2.1]that adualityon a
category𝒞\mathcal{C}is a pair(D,δ)(D,\delta)consisting of a
contravariant functorD:𝒞→𝒞D\colon\,\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}and a natural isomorphismδ:Id𝒞⇒∼D​D\delta\colon\,\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}DDsuch that(𝕀D⋅δ)​(δ⋅𝕀D)=𝕀D(\mathbb{I}_{D}\cdot\delta)\,(\delta\cdot\mathbb{I}_{D})=\mathbb{I}_{D},
i.e., for every objectCCin𝒞\mathcal{C},δD​(C)=D​(δC)−1:D​(C)→D​D​D​(C).\delta_{D(C)}=D(\delta_{C})^{-1}\colon\,D(C)\to DDD(C).

Note that for any duality(D,δ)(D,\delta), the pair(D,−δ)(D,-\delta)also is
a duality.

Amorphismof categories with duality(𝒞1,D1,δ1)→(𝒞2,D2,δ2)(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\to(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})is a pair(R,θ)(R,\theta)consisting of a functorR:𝒞1→𝒞2R\colon\,\mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}_{2}and a natural isomorphismθ:R​D1⇒∼D2​R\theta\colon\,RD_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}D_{2}Rsuch that the following diagram commutes:R\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝕀R⋅δ1\scriptstyle{\mathbb{I}_{R}\cdot\delta_{1}}δ2⋅𝕀R\scriptstyle{\delta_{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{R}}R​D1​D1\textstyle{R{D_{1}}{D_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}θ⋅𝕀D1\scriptstyle{\theta\cdot\mathbb{I}_{D_{1}}}D2​D2​R\textstyle{D_{2}D_{2}R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝕀D2⋅θ\scriptstyle{\mathbb{I}_{D_{2}}\cdot\theta}D2​R​D1\textstyle{D_{2}RD_{1}}

Throughout this section, we only considerkk-linear categories
subject to the same conditions as in §1:kkis a
commutative ring in which22is invertible, and every idempotent
morphism splits. All the functors we consider arekk-linear. A notion
ofsimilitudeof morphisms of
categories with duality is defined as follows: morphismsR~=(R,θ)\widetilde{R}=(R,\theta)andS~=(S,τ)\widetilde{S}=(S,\tau)from(𝒞1,D1,δ1)(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})to(𝒞2,D2,δ2)(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})are said to besimilarif there exists a natural isomorphismρ:R⇒∼S\rho\colon R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}Sand a invertible scalarμ∈k×\mu\in k^{\times}such that the
following diagram commutes:R​D1\textstyle{RD_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}ρ⋅𝕀D1\scriptstyle{\rho\cdot\mathbb{I}_{D_{1}}}θ\scriptstyle{\theta}D2​R\textstyle{D_{2}R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}μ​𝕀D2​R\scriptstyle{\mu\,\mathbb{I}_{D_{2}R}}D2​R\textstyle{D_{2}R}S​D1\textstyle{SD_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}τ\scriptstyle{\tau}D2​S\textstyle{D_{2}S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝕀D2⋅ρ\scriptstyle{\mathbb{I}_{D_{2}}\cdot\rho}

i.e., forC∈𝒞1C\in\mathcal{C}_{1},D2​(ρC)​τC​ρD1​(C)=μ​θC.D_{2}(\rho_{C})\,\tau_{C}\,\rho_{D_{1}(C)}=\mu\,\theta_{C}.

The natural isomorphismρ\rhois then said to be asimilitudeR~⇒S~\widetilde{R}\Rightarrow\widetilde{S}withmultiplierμ\mu. Similitudes
with multiplier11arenatural isomorphismsof morphisms of
categories with duality.

Diagram chase establishes the following multiplicativity property: for
any similitudeρ1:R~1⇒S~1\rho_{1}\colon\widetilde{R}_{1}\Rightarrow\widetilde{S}_{1}of morphisms from(𝒞1,D1,δ1)(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})to(𝒞2,D2,δ2)(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})with multiplierμ1\mu_{1}and any similitudeρ2:R~2⇒S~2\rho_{2}\colon\widetilde{R}_{2}\Rightarrow\widetilde{S}_{2}of morphisms from(𝒞2,D2,δ2)(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})to(𝒞3,D3,δ3)(\mathcal{C}_{3},D_{3},\delta_{3})with multiplierμ2\mu_{2}, the productρ2⋅ρ1\rho_{2}\cdot\rho_{1}is a similitudeR~2​R~1⇒S~2​S~1\widetilde{R}_{2}\widetilde{R}_{1}\Rightarrow\widetilde{S}_{2}\widetilde{S}_{1}with

Note that a commutation between(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)and(D,δ)(D,\delta)also defines a commutation between(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)and(D,−δ)(D,-\delta).
Commuting conjugations and dualities on a category𝒞\mathcal{C}yield new
dualities on𝒞\mathcal{C}and on the double category𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}as follows:

## Proposition 2.1.

Given a conjugation(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)and a duality(D,δ)(D,\delta)with commutationα\alphaon a category𝒞\mathcal{C},
dualities(ι​D,δα)(\iota D,\delta_{\alpha})and(D​ι,δα−1)(D\iota,\delta_{\alpha^{-1}})are defined byδα\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}=(𝕀ι⋅α⋅𝕀D)​(λ−1⋅𝕀D​D)​δ:Id𝒞⇒∼ι​D​ι​D\displaystyle=(\mathbb{I}_{\iota}\cdot\alpha\cdot\mathbb{I}_{D})\,(\lambda^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{DD})\,\delta\colon\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\iota D\iota Dδα−1\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha^{-1}}=(𝕀D⋅α⋅𝕀ι)​(δ⋅𝕀ι​ι)​λ−1:Id𝒞⇒∼D​ι​D​ι.\displaystyle=(\mathbb{I}_{D}\cdot\alpha\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\iota})\,(\delta\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\iota\iota})\,\lambda^{-1}\colon\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}D\iota D\iota.

Moreover,(Id𝒞,α)(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha)is an isomorphism of categories
with duality(𝒞,ι​D,δα)→∼(𝒞,D​ι,δα−1)(\mathcal{C},\iota D,\delta_{\alpha})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{C},D\iota,\delta_{\alpha^{-1}}).

## Proposition 2.2.

Given a conjugation(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)and a duality(D,δ)(D,\delta)with commutationα\alphaon a category𝒞\mathcal{C}, a
duality(D′,δ′)(D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})on the double category𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}is defined as
follows: for every(C,f)∈𝒞′(C,f)\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}and every morphismφ\varphiin𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime},D′​(C,f)=(D​(C),−D​(f)−1​αC),D′​(φ)=D​(φ),δ(C,f)′=δC.D^{\prime}(C,f)=(D(C),-D(f)^{-1}\,\alpha_{C}),\qquad D^{\prime}(\varphi)=D(\varphi),\qquad\delta^{\prime}_{(C,f)}=\delta_{C}.

Moreover, settingα(C,f)′=−αC\alpha^{\prime}_{(C,f)}=-\alpha_{C}for(C,f)∈𝒞′(C,f)\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}defines a natural isomorphismα′:ι′​D′⇒∼D′​ι′,\alpha^{\prime}\colon\,\iota^{\prime}D^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}D^{\prime}\iota^{\prime},

which is a commutation between(ι′,λ′)(\iota^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})and(D′,δ′)(D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}).

We omit the proofs of Propositions2.1and2.2, which consist in lengthy but straightforward
computations.

Recall from §1.1the functorF:𝒞→𝒞′F\colon\,\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.
Define natural isomorphismsϕwidehat:F​D⇒∼D′​Fandϕwidecheck:F​ι​D⇒∼ι′​D′​F\widehat{\phi}\colon\,FD\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}D^{\prime}F\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widecheck{\phi}\colon\,F\iota D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\iota^{\prime}D^{\prime}F

as follows: forC∈𝒞C\in\mathcal{C},ϕwidehatC=(IdD​(C)00−αC)andϕwidecheckC=(Idι​D​(C)00ι​(αC)).\widehat{\phi}_{C}=\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Id}_{D(C)}&0\\
0&-\alpha_{C}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widecheck{\phi}_{C}=\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Id}_{\iota D(C)}&0\\
0&\iota(\alpha_{C})\end{pmatrix}.

Computation shows that the pairsFwidehat=(F,ϕwidehat)\widehat{F}=(F,\widehat{\phi})andFwidecheck=(F,ϕwidecheck)\widecheck{F}=(F,\widecheck{\phi})are
morphisms of categories with dualityFwidehat:(𝒞,D,±δ)→(𝒞′,D′,±δ′)andFwidecheck:(𝒞,ι​D,±δα)→(𝒞′,ι′​D′,±δα′′).\widehat{F}\colon\,(\mathcal{C},D,\pm\delta)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},D^{\prime},\pm\delta^{\prime})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widecheck{F}\colon\,(\mathcal{C},\iota D,\pm\delta_{\alpha})\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}D^{\prime},\pm\delta^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}}).

Iterating theFwidehat\widehat{F}construction, we obtain an infinite
sequence of
morphisms of categories with duality:(2.2)(𝒞,D,δ)→Fwidehat(𝒞′,D′,δ′)→Fwidehat′(𝒞′′,D′′,δ′′)→Fwidehat′′⋯(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)\xrightarrow{\widehat{F}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\widehat{F}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow{\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}}\cdots

Morphisms in the reverse direction are provided by the
forgetful functorG:𝒞′→𝒞G\colon\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}, but they involve different
dualities: it is easily checked that
natural isomorphismsγwidehat:G​D′⇒∼ι​D​G\widehat{\gamma}\colon GD^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\iota DGandγwidecheck:G​ι′​D′⇒∼D​G\widecheck{\gamma}\colon G\iota^{\prime}D^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}DGare defined byγwidehat(C,f)=αC−1​D​(f)andγwidecheck(C,f)=D​(f−1)​αCfor(C,f)∈𝒞′.\widehat{\gamma}_{(C,f)}=\alpha_{C}^{-1}\,D(f)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widecheck{\gamma}_{(C,f)}=D(f^{-1})\,\alpha_{C}\qquad\text{for $(C,f)\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}$}.

The pairsGwidehat=(G,γwidehat)\widehat{G}=(G,\widehat{\gamma})andGwidecheck=(G,γwidecheck)\widecheck{G}=(G,\widecheck{\gamma})are morphisms of categories with duality:Gwidehat:(𝒞′,D′,±δ′)→(𝒞,ι​D,∓δα)andGwidecheck:(𝒞′,ι′​D′,±δα′′)→(𝒞,D,±δ).\widehat{G}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},D^{\prime},\pm\delta^{\prime})\to(\mathcal{C},\iota D,\mp\delta_{\alpha})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widecheck{G}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}D^{\prime},\pm\delta^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}})\to(\mathcal{C},D,\pm\delta).

This change of dualities (and signs) explains why the
sequence (2.2)
turns out to be88-periodic, in contrast with the22-periodicity of
the sequence (1.10).

## 2.2.Periodicity

Continuing with the same notation as in the preceding section, we turn
the equivalence of categories𝒞≡𝒞′′\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}afforded by the
functorsΘ\ThetaandΨ\Psiof §1.2into
equivalences of categories with duality.

-\alpha_{C}&0\end{pmatrix}.

## Proof.

Recall from (1.5) the natural isomorphismξ:Id𝒞⇒∼Θ​Ψ\xi\colon\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\Theta\Psi; computation shows that(𝕀D⋅ξ)​(θwidecheck⋅𝕀Ψ)​(𝕀Θ⋅ψwidehat)​(ξ⋅𝕀D)=𝕀D​and​(𝕀ι​D⋅ξ)​(θwidehat⋅𝕀Ψ)​(𝕀Θ⋅ψwidecheck)​(ξ⋅𝕀ι​D)=𝕀ι​D.(\mathbb{I}_{D}\cdot\xi)\,(\widecheck{\theta}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\Psi})\,(\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}\cdot\widehat{\psi})\,(\xi\cdot\mathbb{I}_{D})=\mathbb{I}_{D}\text{ and }(\mathbb{I}_{\iota D}\cdot\xi)\,(\widehat{\theta}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\Psi})\,(\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}\cdot\widecheck{\psi})\,(\xi\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\iota D})=\mathbb{I}_{\iota D}.

Therefore,ξ\xidefines natural isomorphisms(Id𝒞,𝕀D)⇒∼Θwidecheck​Ψwidehat(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathbb{I}_{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\widecheck{\Theta}\widehat{\Psi}and(Id𝒞,𝕀ι​D)⇒∼Θwidehat​Ψwidecheck(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathbb{I}_{\iota D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\widehat{\Theta}\widecheck{\Psi}.

## 3.Symmetric spaces

As in the previous sections,𝒞\mathcal{C}denotes a pseudo-abeliankk-linear category for
an arbitrary commutative ringkkin which22is
invertible. Throughout the section,(D,δ)(D,\delta)is a duality on𝒞\mathcal{C}.

## 3.1.Definitions

Asymmetric spacein the category with duality(𝒞,D,δ)(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)is a pair(C,h)(C,h)consisting of an objectC∈𝒞C\in\mathcal{C}and
an isomorphismh:C→D​(C)h\colon C\to D(C)in𝒞\mathcal{C}such thatD​(h)​δC=hD(h)\,\delta_{C}=h. Forμ∈k×\mu\in k^{\times}, asimilitude with
multiplierμ\mufrom a symmetric space(C1,h1)(C_{1},h_{1})to a symmetric
space(C2,h2)(C_{2},h_{2})is an isomorphismφ:C1→∼C2\varphi\colon C_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{2}in𝒞\mathcal{C}such that the following diagram
commutes:C1\textstyle{C_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}φ\scriptstyle{\varphi}h1\scriptstyle{h_{1}}C2\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}h2\scriptstyle{h_{2}}D​(C1)\textstyle{D(C_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}μ​IdD​(C1)\scriptstyle{\mu\operatorname{Id}_{D(C_{1})}}D​(C1)\textstyle{D(C_{1})}D​(C2)\textstyle{D(C_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}D​(φ)\scriptstyle{D(\varphi)}

Similitudes with multiplier11areisometries.

ForL∈𝒞L\in\mathcal{C}, letH​(L)H(L)denote the symmetric spaceH​(L)=(L⊕D​(L),(0IdD​(L)δL0)).H(L)=\Bigl(L\oplus D(L),\;\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\operatorname{Id}_{D(L)}\\
\delta_{L}&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\Bigr).

By definition, a symmetric space(C,h)(C,h)ishyperbolicif
it is isometric toH​(L)H(L)for someL∈𝒞L\in\mathcal{C}. Note that for everyμ∈k×\mu\in k^{\times}the morphism(μ​IdL00IdD​(L))\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\mu\operatorname{Id}_{L}&0\\
0&\operatorname{Id}_{D(L)}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)is a similitudeH​(L)→H​(L)H(L)\to H(L)with multiplierμ\mu. Therefore,
hyperbolicity is preserved under similitude.

## 3.2.Hermitian categories

Let𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)denote the category whose objects are
symmetric spaces in(𝒞,D,δ)(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)and morphisms are
isometries. We refer to𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)as thehermitian
categoryof the category with duality(𝒞,D,δ)(\mathcal{C},D,\delta).
Every morphism of categories with dualityR~=(R,θ):(𝒞1,D1,δ1)→(𝒞2,D2,δ2)\widetilde{R}=(R,\theta)\colon(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\to(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})induces a functor𝒮​(R~):𝒮​(𝒞1,D1,δ1)→𝒮​(𝒞2,D2,δ2)\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{R})\colon\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})

mapping(C,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞1,D1,δ1)(C,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})to𝒮​(R~)​(C,h)=(R​(C),θC​R​(h)),\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{R})(C,h)=\bigl(R(C),\theta_{C}\,R(h)\bigr),

see[11, II(2.6)]or[2, Def. 11]. It readily follows
from the definitions that𝒮​(R~)\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{R})maps hyperbolic
symmetric spaces to hyperbolic symmetric spaces, because for everyL∈𝒞1L\in\mathcal{C}_{1}the morphism(IdR​(L)00θL):R​(L)⊕R​D1​(L)→R​(L)⊕D2​R​(L)\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\operatorname{Id}_{R(L)}&0\\
0&\theta_{L}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\colon R(L)\oplus RD_{1}(L)\to R(L)\oplus D_{2}R(L)is an isometry
from𝒮​(R~)​(H​(L))\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{R})\bigl(H(L)\bigr)toH​R​(L)HR(L).

Ifρ:R~⇒∼S~\rho\colon\widetilde{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\widetilde{S}is a similitude with multiplierμ∈k×\mu\in k^{\times}between morphisms of categories with dualityR~\widetilde{R},S~:(𝒞1,D1,δ1)→(𝒞2,D2,δ2)\widetilde{S}\colon(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\to(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2}),
then for every symmetric space(C,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞1,D1,h1)(C,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},h_{1})the
isomorphismρC:R​(C)→S​(C)\rho_{C}\colon R(C)\to S(C)in𝒞2\mathcal{C}_{2}defines a
similitude with multiplierμ\mu𝒮​(ρC):𝒮​(R~)​(C,h)→𝒮​(S~)​(C,h).\mathcal{S}(\rho_{C})\colon\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{R})(C,h)\to\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{S})(C,h).

The main result of this section is as follows:

## Theorem 3.1.

Under the composition of two consecutive functors in the
octagon (3.2), the image of each category consists
of hyperbolic symmetric spaces.

Since hyperbolicity is preserved by similitude
and by𝒮​(Θwidehat)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{\Theta})and𝒮​(Θwidecheck)\mathcal{S}(\widecheck{\Theta}), we may
substitute in the theorem the functors
in the upper row of (3.1) for the
functors𝒮​(Fwidehat)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F}),𝒮​(Gwidehat)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{G}),𝒮​(Fwidecheck)\mathcal{S}(\widecheck{F}),𝒮​(Gwidecheck)\mathcal{S}(\widecheck{G})from (3.2). Moreover,

of𝒮​(Fwidehat)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})and𝒮​(Fwidehat′)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F}^{\prime}). Therefore, the
theorem follows from the following proposition:

## Proposition 3.2.

The composition𝒮​(F𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒ℎ𝑎𝑡′)​𝒮​(F𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒ℎ𝑎𝑡)=𝒮​(F𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒ℎ𝑎𝑡′​F𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒ℎ𝑎𝑡)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F}^{\prime})\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})=\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F}^{\prime}\widehat{F})maps every symmetric space in𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)to a hyperbolic symmetric space in𝒮​(𝒞′′,D′′,δ′′)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}).

The following lemma characterises the symmetric spaces in the image of𝒮​(Fwidehat)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F}). It is to be used in the proofs of
Proposition3.2and Theorems4.6and4.8.

(C′,h′)≃𝒮​(Fwidehat)​(X,h)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})\simeq\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})(X,h)for some symmetric space(X,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)(X,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta);

there exists an isometryθ′:(ι′​(C′),αC′′​ι′​(h′))→(C′,h′)\theta^{\prime}\colon\bigl(\iota^{\prime}(C^{\prime}),\alpha^{\prime}_{C^{\prime}}\,\iota^{\prime}(h^{\prime})\bigr)\to(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})in𝒮​(𝒞′,D′,δ′)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})such thatθ′​ι′​(θ′)=λC′′\theta^{\prime}\,\iota^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})=\lambda^{\prime}_{C^{\prime}}.

## Proof of Proposition3.2.

Let(C′,h′)∈𝒮​(𝒞′,D′,δ′)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})be such that(C′,h′)=𝒮​(Fwidehat)​(X,h)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})(X,h)for some(X,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)(X,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta). Lemma3.3yields an
isometryθ′:(ι′​(C′),αC′′​ι′​(h′))→(C′,h′)\theta^{\prime}\colon\bigl(\iota^{\prime}(C^{\prime}),\alpha^{\prime}_{C^{\prime}}\,\iota^{\prime}(h^{\prime})\bigr)\to(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})such thatθ′​ι′​(θ′)=λC′′\theta^{\prime}\,\iota^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime})=\lambda^{\prime}_{C^{\prime}}, hence an objectC′′=(C′,θ′)∈𝒞′′C^{\prime\prime}=(C^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}. In𝒞′′\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}, the morphism(IdC′θ′12​h′−12​h′​θ′):F′​(C′)→C′′⊕D′′​(C′′)\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Id}_{C^{\prime}}&\theta^{\prime}\\
\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\prime}&-\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\prime}\,\theta^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\colon F^{\prime}(C^{\prime})\to C^{\prime\prime}\oplus D^{\prime\prime}(C^{\prime\prime})

is an isometry𝒮​(Fwidehat′)​(C′,h′)→H​(C′′)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F}^{\prime})(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})\to H(C^{\prime\prime}).
∎

## 3.3.Example: Hermitian spaces

For the typical dualities on categories of projective modules
discussed in
§2.3, the symmetric spaces are (slightly generalised)
hermitian spaces. Let(σ,ε)(\sigma,\varepsilon)be an antistructure on akk-algebraAAand let(D,δ)(D,\delta)be the derived duality on𝒫A\mathcal{P}_{A}. To every(P,h)∈𝒮​(𝒫A,D,δ)(P,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A},D,\delta)we attach a mapP×P→AP\times P\to A, for which we
use the same notationhh, defined byh​(x,y)=⟨h​(x),y⟩forx,y∈P.h(x,y)=\langle h(x),y\rangle\quad\text{for $x$, $y\in P$.}

It readily follows from the definitions thathhis a
nonsingular sesquilinear map for the antiautomorphismσ\sigmaonAA,
and the propertyh=D​(h)​δPh=D(h)\,\delta_{P}means thath​(y,x)=σ​(h​(x,y))​εforx,y∈P.h(y,x)=\sigma\bigl(h(x,y)\bigr)\,\varepsilon\qquad\text{for $x$, $y\in P$.}

In C.T.C. Wall’s terminology[23]the maph:P×P→Ah\colon P\times P\to Ais a nonsingular(σ,ε)(\sigma,\varepsilon)-reflexive
form. Whenε\varepsilonlies in the center ofAA, thenσ\sigmais an involution and𝒮​(𝒫A,D,δ)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A},D,\delta)is the category ofε\varepsilon-hermitianAA-modules as defined in[11, p. 12](and denoted there byℌε​(A)\mathfrak{H}^{\varepsilon}(A)). Abusing
terminology, we also call(σ,ε)(\sigma,\varepsilon)-hermitian
modulesthe symmetric spaces in𝒮​(𝒫A,D,δ)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A},D,\delta)when(D,δ)(D,\delta)is derived from the antistructure(σ,ε)(\sigma,\varepsilon),
and call(σ,ε)(\sigma,\varepsilon)-hermitian formsC.T.C. Wall’s(σ,ε)(\sigma,\varepsilon)-reflexive forms.
We write𝒮​(𝒫A,D,δ)=ℋε​(A,σ)and𝒮​(𝒫A,D,−δ)=ℋ−ε​(A,σ).\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A},D,\delta)=\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(A,\sigma)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A},D,-\delta)=\mathcal{H}^{-\varepsilon}(A,\sigma).

Similarly, given a commutationα\alphabetween(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)and(D,δ)(D,\delta)as in §2.3, we attach to every
symmetric space(P,h)∈𝒮​(𝒫A,ι​D,δα)(P,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A},\iota D,\delta_{\alpha})a maph:P×P→Adefined byh​(x,y)={h​(x),y}forx,y∈P.h\colon P\times P\to A\quad\text{defined by}\quad h(x,y)=\{h(x),y\}\quad\text{for $x$, $y\in P$.}

This map is a nonsingular(σ​ι,εα)(\sigma\iota,\varepsilon_{\alpha})-hermitian
form, hence𝒮​(𝒫A,ι​D,δα)=ℋεα​(A,σ​ι)and𝒮​(𝒫A,ι​D,−δα)=ℋ−εα​(A,σ​ι).\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A},\iota D,\delta_{\alpha})=\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon_{\alpha}}(A,\sigma\iota)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A},\iota D,-\delta_{\alpha})=\mathcal{H}^{-\varepsilon_{\alpha}}(A,\sigma\iota).

Likewise, for the dualities(D′,±δ′)(D^{\prime},\pm\delta^{\prime})and(ι′​D′,±δα′′)(\iota^{\prime}D^{\prime},\pm\delta^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}})derived from(σ′,±ε′)(\sigma^{\prime},\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})and(σ′​ι′,±εα)(\sigma^{\prime}\iota^{\prime},\pm\varepsilon_{\alpha}),𝒮​(𝒫A′,D′,±δ′)=ℋ±ε​(A′,σ′)and𝒮​(𝒫A′,ι′​D′,±δα′′)=ℋ±εα​(A′,σ′​ι′).\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A^{\prime}},D^{\prime},\pm\delta^{\prime})=\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(A^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{A^{\prime}},\iota^{\prime}D^{\prime},\pm\delta^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}})=\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon_{\alpha}}(A^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\iota^{\prime}).

The functors𝒮​(Fwidehat):ℋ±ε​(A,σ)→ℋ±ε​(A′,σ′)and𝒮​(Fwidecheck):ℋ±εα​(A,σ​ι)→ℋ±εα​(A′,σ′​ι′)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})\colon\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(A,\sigma)\to\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(A^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{S}(\widecheck{F})\colon\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon_{\alpha}}(A,\sigma\iota)\to\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon_{\alpha}}(A^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\iota^{\prime})

are given by scalar extensions, mapping
every(σ,±ε)(\sigma,\pm\varepsilon)- or(σ​ι,±εα)(\sigma\iota,\pm\varepsilon_{\alpha})-hermitian space(P,h)(P,h)to(P⊗AA′,h′)(P\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},h^{\prime})defined byh′​(x1⊗1+x2⊗j,y1⊗1+y2⊗j)=h​(x1,y1)+σ′​(j)​h​(x2,y2)​j+h​(x1,y2)​j+σ′​(j)​h​(x2,y1)=h​(x1,y1)−u​ι−1​(h​(x2,y2))+(h​(x1,y2)−u​ι−1​(h​(x2,y1))​z−1)​jh^{\prime}(x_{1}\otimes 1+x_{2}\otimes j,\,y_{1}\otimes 1+y_{2}\otimes j)=h(x_{1},y_{1})+\sigma^{\prime}(j)h(x_{2},y_{2})j+h(x_{1},y_{2})j+\sigma^{\prime}(j)h(x_{2},y_{1})\\

forx1x_{1},x2x_{2},y1y_{1},y2∈Py_{2}\in P.

This octagon is only superficially different from the one
in[6, (3.1)], which hasℋ∓ε​(B,τ1)\mathcal{H}^{\mp\varepsilon}(B,\tau_{1})forℋ±ε​(B,τ1)\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(B,\tau_{1})andℋ±ε​(A,σ)\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(A,\sigma)forℋ∓ε​(A,σ​Int⁡(η​ζ))\mathcal{H}^{\mp\varepsilon}(A,\sigma\,\operatorname{Int}(\eta\zeta)). The
differences are resolved by scaling: if(Q,g)∈ℋ±ε​(B,τ1)(Q,g)\in\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(B,\tau_{1}),
then(Q,ζ​g)∈ℋ∓ε​(B,τ1)(Q,\zeta g)\in\mathcal{H}^{\mp\varepsilon}(B,\tau_{1}), and if(P,h)∈ℋ∓ε​(A,σ​Int⁡(η​ζ))(P,h)\in\mathcal{H}^{\mp\varepsilon}(A,\sigma\,\operatorname{Int}(\eta\zeta)), then(P,ζ​η​h)∈ℋ±ε​(A,σ)(P,\zeta\eta h)\in\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(A,\sigma).

Note that, by the very nature of its periodic construction, the same
octagon can still be obtained with a shift of one edge by starting
with the algebraAA. To see this explicitly, letιA=IdA\iota_{A}=\operatorname{Id}_{A}andλA=ζ2∈k×\lambda_{A}=\zeta^{2}\in k^{\times}, and setK=k⊕k​jK=k\oplus kjwithj2=λAj^{2}=\lambda_{A}, a quadratic étalekk-algebra with nontrivial
automorphism𝖼𝖺𝗇K/k\mathsf{can}_{K/k}. The(ιA,λA)(\iota_{A},\lambda_{A})-twisted
quadratic extension ofAAisA′=A⊗kKA^{\prime}=A\otimes_{k}K, with the automorphismιA′=IdA⊗𝖼𝖺𝗇K/k\iota^{\prime}_{A}=\operatorname{Id}_{A}\otimes\mathsf{can}_{K/k}. The scalarλA\lambda_{A}satisfies the conditions (i)—(iii) onuuin §2.3,
hence it yields a commutationαA\alpha_{A}between the conjugation on𝒫A\mathcal{P}_{A}derived from(ιA,λA)(\iota_{A},\lambda_{A})and the duality derived
from(σ,ε)(\sigma,\varepsilon). With this choice,εαA=ε\varepsilon_{\alpha_{A}}=\varepsilonand the involutionsσ′\sigma^{\prime}andσ′​ιA′\sigma^{\prime}\iota^{\prime}_{A}onA′A^{\prime}areσ⊗𝖼𝖺𝗇K/k\sigma\otimes\mathsf{can}_{K/k}andσ⊗IdK\sigma\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{K}respectively. In order to relate the
resulting octagon (3.7) with (3.8) or[6, (3.1)], observe thate=12​(1+ζ⊗j−1)∈A′e=\frac{1}{2}(1+\zeta\otimes j^{-1})\in A^{\prime}is an idempotent centralised byBB. Mappingb∈Bb\in Btoe​(b⊗1)​ee(b\otimes 1)eidentifiesBBwithe​A′​e≃EndA′⁡(e​A′)eA^{\prime}e\simeq\operatorname{End}_{A^{\prime}}(eA^{\prime}). Following[11, I(9.1)],
Morita theory yields an equivalence of categories𝒫B→∼𝒫A′\mathcal{P}_{B}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}_{A^{\prime}}mappingP∈𝒫BP\in\mathcal{P}_{B}toP⊗Be​A′P\otimes_{B}eA^{\prime}. Moreover, equivalences of categories𝖬1:ℋ±ε​(B,τ1)→∼ℋ±ε​(A′,σ′)and𝖬2:ℋ±ε​(B,τ2)→∼ℋ∓ε​(A′,σ′​ι′)\mathsf{M}_{1}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(B,\tau_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(A^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{M}_{2}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon}(B,\tau_{2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{H}^{\mp\varepsilon}(A^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\iota^{\prime})

can be obtained by hermitian form composition with the mapsh1h_{1},h2:e​A′×e​A′→A′h_{2}\colon eA^{\prime}\times eA^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}defined byh1​(x,y)=σ′​(x)​yandh2​(x,y)=σ′​ι′​(x)​η​yforx,y∈e​A′.h_{1}(x,y)=\sigma^{\prime}(x)y\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{2}(x,y)=\sigma^{\prime}\iota^{\prime}(x)\eta y\qquad\text{for $x$, $y\in eA^{\prime}$}.

## 4.Witt groups

Throughout this section,𝒞\mathcal{C}is, as in the preceding sections, a
pseudo-abeliankk-linear category wherekkis a commutative ring in
which22is invertible. We assume moreover that𝒞\mathcal{C}is
essentially small, which means that the isomorphism classes of objects
in𝒞\mathcal{C}form a set. If𝒞\mathcal{C}carries a conjugation(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)or a duality(D,δ)(D,\delta), it follows that the
categories𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}and𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)also are essentially
small. If(𝒞,D,δ)(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)is anexactcategory with duality
in the sense of[2, §1.2.2], thenmetabolicsymmetric spaces are defined, and a classical construction due to
Knebusch[2, Def. 27]yields theWitt groupW​(𝒞,D,δ)W(\mathcal{C},D,\delta): this group is the quotient of the monoid of
isometry classes of symmetric spaces in𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)by the
submonoid of isometry classes of metabolic symmetric spaces. Thus, the
definition ofW​(𝒞,D,δ)W(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)depends on additional structure
on the category with duality(𝒞,D,δ)(\mathcal{C},D,\delta).

If(𝒞1,D1,δ1)(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})and(𝒞2,D2,δ2)(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})are exact
categories with duality andR~=(R,θ):(𝒞1,D1,δ1)→(𝒞2,D2,δ2)\widetilde{R}=(R,\theta)\colon(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\to(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})is a morphism of
categories with duality withRRan exact functor, then it follows
from the definitions that the functor𝒮​(R~):𝒮​(𝒞1,D1,δ1)→𝒮​(𝒞2,D2,δ2)\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{R})\colon\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})induces a
group homomorphismW​(R~):W​(𝒞1,D1,δ1)→W​(𝒞2,D2,δ2)W(\widetilde{R})\colon W(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\to W(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2}); and if two morphismsR~\widetilde{R},S~:(𝒞1,D1,δ1)→(𝒞2,D2,δ2)\widetilde{S}\colon(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\to(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})preserving the exact structures are related by a similitudeρ:R~⇒∼S~\rho\colon\widetilde{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\widetilde{S}with multiplierμ∈k×\mu\in k^{\times}, then the similitude𝒮​(ρ):𝒮​(R~)⇒∼𝒮​(S~)\mathcal{S}(\rho)\colon\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{S})defines an isomorphism⟨μ⟩\langle\mu\ranglemapping the Witt class of(C,h)∈𝒮​(C2,D2,δ2)(C,h)\in\mathcal{S}(C_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})to the Witt class of(C,μ​h)(C,\mu h),
which fits in
the following commutative diagram:W​(𝒞1,D1,δ1)\textstyle{W(\mathcal{C}_{1},D_{1},\delta_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}W​(R~)\scriptstyle{W(\widetilde{R})}W​(S~)\scriptstyle{W(\widetilde{S})}W​(𝒞2,D2,δ2)\textstyle{W(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}⟨μ⟩\scriptstyle{\langle\mu\rangle}W​(𝒞2,D2,δ2)\textstyle{W(\mathcal{C}_{2},D_{2},\delta_{2})}

In this section, we consider two cases where Knebusch’s construction
turns the octagon (3.2) of hermitian categories into
an88-periodic chain complex of Witt groups: the split exact
structure and the usual exact structure in abelian categories. In each
case, the crucial property that determines the exactness of the
resulting octagon of Witt groups is the following:

## Property (P).

Let(C,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)(C,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)be a symmetric space. If𝒮​(Fwidehat)​(C,h)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})(C,h)is metabolic, then there exists an isometryθ:(ι​(C),αC​ι​(h))→(C,h)\theta\colon\bigl(\iota(C),\alpha_{C}\,\iota(h)\bigr)\to(C,h)such thatθ​ι​(θ)=λC\theta\,\iota(\theta)=\lambda_{C}.

When this property holds for representatives of each Witt class in the
Witt groups involved in the octagon, exactness
follows from Lemma3.3.

## Remark 4.1.

Note that𝒮​(Fwidehat)​(C,h)=(F​(C),ϕwidehatC​F​(h))\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})(C,h)=(F(C),\widehat{\phi}_{C}\,F(h))with(G​F​(C),G​(ϕwidehatC​F​(h)))=(C,h)⟂(ι​(C),−αC​ι​(h))(GF(C),G(\widehat{\phi}_{C}\,F(h)))=(C,h)\perp(\iota(C),-\alpha_{C}\,\iota(h)). When Witt
cancellation holds in𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta), there exists an
isometry from(ι​(C),αC​ι​(h))(\iota(C),\alpha_{C}\iota(h))to(C,h)(C,h)if and only if(C,h)⟂(ι​(C),−αC​ι​(h))≃(C,h)⟂(C,−h)(C,h)\perp(\iota(C),-\alpha_{C}\,\iota(h))\simeq(C,h)\perp(C,-h), i.e.,G​𝒮​(Fwidehat)​(C,h)≃H​(C)G\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})(C,h)\simeq H(C).

## 4.1.The split exact structure

In this case, the exact sequences are the split sequences, hence every
additive functor preserves the exact structure. Since22is invertible inkk, the metabolic spaces are
the hyperbolic spaces, see[2, Example 21]. Therefore, the
Witt groupW​(𝒞,D,δ)W(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)is the
usual Witt group as defined in[21, Ch. 7, §2].

In this subsection, we provide conditions under which this chain
complex is an exact sequence.

For every symmetric space(C,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)(C,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)the
endomorphismkk-algebraEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)carries an involutionσh\sigma_{h}(i.e., an anti-automorphism of period22) defined byσh​(φ)=h−1​D​(φ)​hforφ∈End𝒞⁡(C).\sigma_{h}(\varphi)=h^{-1}\,D(\varphi)\,h\qquad\text{for $\varphi\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)$}.

The symmetric space(C,h)(C,h)is said to besplit anisotropicif
there is no nonzero direct summandt:L→Ct\colon L\to Csuch thatD​(t)​h​t=0D(t)\,h\,t=0or, equivalently since𝒞\mathcal{C}is
pseudo-abelian, if there is no nonzero idempotente∈End𝒞⁡(C)e\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)such thatσh​(e)​e=0\sigma_{h}(e)\,e=0. Recall thatEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)is said
to besemilocalif factoring out its Jacobson radicalRRyields akk-algebraEnd𝒞⁡(C)/R\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)/Rthat is right artinian; it israd-adically completeif it is complete for theRR-adic
topology (in which(Rn)n∈ℕ(R^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}is a basis of
neighborhoods of0), see[11, II(4.5)]or[12, §21]. For example, right artinian algebras are semilocal
and rad-adically complete since their Jacobson radical is nilpotent,
see[12, (4.12)].

The next lemma demonstrates how these properties are used in the proof
of Property (P).

## Lemma 4.2.

Let(C,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)(C,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)be a split anisotropic symmetric
space. IfEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)is semilocal and rad-adically complete,
then everyφ∈End𝒞⁡(C)\varphi\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)such thatσh​(φ)=IdC−φ\sigma_{h}(\varphi)=\operatorname{Id}_{C}-\varphiis invertible.

## Proof.

LetRRbe the Jacobson radical ofEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)andEnd𝒞⁡(C)¯=End𝒞⁡(C)/R\overline{\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)}=\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)/R. We writeφ¯\overline{\varphi}for the image ofφ∈End𝒞⁡(C)\varphi\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)inEnd𝒞⁡(C)¯\overline{\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)}. Properties of the Jacobson radical imply
thatφ\varphiis invertible ifφ¯\overline{\varphi}is invertible
(see[11, II(4.2.2)]or[12, (4.8)]), and that
idempotents inEnd𝒞⁡(C)¯\overline{\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)}lift to idempotents inEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)whenEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)is rad-adically complete
(see[11, II(4.5.4)]or[12, (21.31)]). Sinceσh\sigma_{h}is an anti-automorphism, it preservesRRand induces an
involutionσh¯\overline{\sigma_{h}}onEnd𝒞⁡(C)¯\overline{\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)}.

As a first step in the proof, we show that ifε∈End𝒞⁡(C)¯\varepsilon\in\overline{\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)}is an idempotent such
thatσh¯​(ε)​ε=0\overline{\sigma_{h}}(\varepsilon)\varepsilon=0, thenε=0\varepsilon=0. This follows from the hypothesis that(C,h)(C,h)is
split anisotropic by the reduction theory in[11, II.4]or[21, Ch. 7, §4]; the following is an easy direct
proof.

Lete0∈End𝒞⁡(C)e_{0}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)be an idempotent such thate0¯=ε\overline{e_{0}}=\varepsilon. We construct a sequence of
idempotents(en)n∈ℕ(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}inEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)such thaten+1≡enmodRn+1e_{n+1}\equiv e_{n}\bmod R^{n+1}andσh​(en)​en∈Rn+1\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n}\in R^{n+1}for alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}: ifene_{n}is given, thenIdC+12​σh​(en)​en\operatorname{Id}_{C}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n}is invertible sinceIdC+12​σh​(en)​en¯=IdC¯\overline{\operatorname{Id}_{C}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{h}(e_{n})e_{n}}=\overline{\operatorname{Id}_{C}}. We may therefore seten+1=(IdC+12​σh​(en)​en)−1​en​(IdC+12​σh​(en)​en).e_{n+1}=(\operatorname{Id}_{C}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n})^{-1}\,e_{n}\,(\operatorname{Id}_{C}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n}).

This is clearly an idempotent, anden+1≡enmodRn+1e_{n+1}\equiv e_{n}\bmod R^{n+1}since12​σh​(en)​en∈Rn+1\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n}\in R^{n+1}. This
last property also impliesen+1≡(IdC−12​σh​(en)​en)​en​(IdC+12​σh​(en)​en)≡en−12​σh​(en)​en+12​en​σh​(en)​enmodRn+2,e_{n+1}\equiv(\operatorname{Id}_{C}-\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n})\,e_{n}\,(\operatorname{Id}_{C}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n})\\
\equiv e_{n}-\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}e_{n}\,\sigma_{h}(e_{n})\,e_{n}\bmod R^{n+2},

whenceσh​(en+1)​en+1∈Rn+2\sigma_{h}(e_{n+1})\,e_{n+1}\in R^{n+2}. SinceEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)is assumed to beRR-adically complete, there
existse∞∈End𝒞⁡(C)e_{\infty}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)such thate∞≡enmodRn+1e_{\infty}\equiv e_{n}\bmod R^{n+1}for alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}. This endomorphism is
idempotent andσh​(e∞)​e∞=0\sigma_{h}(e_{\infty})\,e_{\infty}=0. But(C,h)(C,h)is assumed to be split anisotropic, hencee∞=0e_{\infty}=0and
thereforeε=0\varepsilon=0.

Now, supposeφ∈End𝒞⁡(C)\varphi\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)is such thatσh​(φ)=IdC−φ\sigma_{h}(\varphi)=\operatorname{Id}_{C}-\varphi, henceσh¯​(φ¯)=IdC¯−φ¯\overline{\sigma_{h}}(\overline{\varphi})=\overline{\operatorname{Id}_{C}}-\overline{\varphi}. The Wedderburn–Artin structure theorem
(see[12, (3.5)]) shows thatEnd𝒞⁡(C)¯\overline{\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)}is
isomorphic to a direct product of matrix algebras over division
algebras. Therefore,φ¯\overline{\varphi}is invertible unless there
exists a nonzero idempotentε∈End𝒞⁡(C)¯\varepsilon\in\overline{\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)}such thatφ¯​ε=0\overline{\varphi}\,\varepsilon=0. But ifφ¯​ε=0\overline{\varphi}\,\varepsilon=0, thenσh¯​(ε)​ε=σh¯​(ε)​(IdC¯−φ¯)​ε=σh¯​(φ¯​ε)​ε=0.\overline{\sigma_{h}}(\varepsilon)\varepsilon=\overline{\sigma_{h}}(\varepsilon)\,(\overline{\operatorname{Id}_{C}}-\overline{\varphi})\,\varepsilon=\overline{\sigma_{h}}(\overline{\varphi}\,\varepsilon)\varepsilon=0.

We just saw that this impliesε=0\varepsilon=0, henceφ¯\overline{\varphi}is invertible andφ\varphiis invertible.
∎

## Proposition 4.3.

Property (P) holds for(C,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)(C,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)if(C,h)(C,h)is
split anisotropic andEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)is semilocal and rad-adically
complete.

## Proof.

Let𝒮​(Fwidehat)​(C,h)=(F​(C),h′)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F})(C,h)=(F(C),h^{\prime})whereh′=ϕwidehatC​F​(h)=(h00−αC​ι​(h))h^{\prime}=\widehat{\phi}_{C}\,F(h)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}h&0\\
0&-\alpha_{C}\,\iota(h)\end{smallmatrix}\bigr). Assume(F​(C),h′)≃H​(L′)(F(C),h^{\prime})\simeq H(L^{\prime})for someL′∈𝒞′L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime},
henceF​(C)F(C)may be identified withL′⊕D′​(L′)L^{\prime}\oplus D^{\prime}(L^{\prime}). Projection onL′L^{\prime}yields an
idempotente∈End𝒞′⁡(F​(C))e\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(F(C))such thatIdF​(C)−e\operatorname{Id}_{F(C)}-eis
the projection onD′​(L′)D^{\prime}(L^{\prime}). The involution adjoint to the hyperbolic
form onL′⊕D′​(L′)L^{\prime}\oplus D^{\prime}(L^{\prime})interchanges the projections onL′L^{\prime}and
onD′​(L′)D^{\prime}(L^{\prime}), henceσh′​(e)=IdF​(C)−e\sigma_{h^{\prime}}(e)=\operatorname{Id}_{F(C)}-e. ViewingEnd𝒞′⁡(F​(C))⊂End𝒞⁡(C⊕ι​(C))\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(F(C))\subset\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C\oplus\iota(C)), we may
writee=(φ0φ1φ2φ3)e=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{0}&\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{2}&\varphi_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)for someφ0∈End𝒞⁡(C)\varphi_{0}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C),φ1∈Hom𝒞⁡(ι​(C),C)\varphi_{1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\iota(C),C),φ2∈Hom𝒞⁡(C,ι​(C))\varphi_{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(C,\iota(C))andφ3∈End𝒞⁡(ι​(C))\varphi_{3}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\iota(C)). Becauseeeis a morphism in𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}, we haveφ2=ι​(φ1)​λC−1\varphi_{2}=\iota(\varphi_{1})\,\lambda_{C}^{-1}andφ3=ι​(φ0)\varphi_{3}=\iota(\varphi_{0}). The equatione2=ee^{2}=ethen yields(4.2)φ02+φ1​ι​(φ1)​λC−1=φ0andφ0​φ1+φ1​ι​(φ0)=φ1.\varphi_{0}^{2}+\varphi_{1}\,\iota(\varphi_{1})\,\lambda_{C}^{-1}=\varphi_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{0}\,\varphi_{1}+\varphi_{1}\,\iota(\varphi_{0})=\varphi_{1}.

On the other hand, the conditionσh′​(e)=IdF​(C)−e\sigma_{h^{\prime}}(e)=\operatorname{Id}_{F(C)}-eyields(4.3)σh​(φ0)=IdC−φ0andh−1​D​(ι​(φ1)​λC−1)​αC​ι​(h)=φ1.\sigma_{h}(\varphi_{0})=\operatorname{Id}_{C}-\varphi_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad h^{-1}\,D(\iota(\varphi_{1})\,\lambda_{C}^{-1})\,\alpha_{C}\,\iota(h)=\varphi_{1}.

Using the first equation in (4.3), we may rewrite the
equations in (4.2) as(4.4)φ1​ι​(φ1)​λC−1=σh​(φ0)​φ0andφ1​ι​(φ0)=σh​(φ0)​φ1.\varphi_{1}\,\iota(\varphi_{1})\,\lambda_{C}^{-1}=\sigma_{h}(\varphi_{0})\,\varphi_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{1}\,\iota(\varphi_{0})=\sigma_{h}(\varphi_{0})\,\varphi_{1}.

Moreover, the first equation in (4.3) shows by
Lemma4.2thatφ0\varphi_{0}is invertible, hence it
follows from the first equation in (4.4) thatφ1\varphi_{1}is
also invertible. We may therefore define an isomorphismθ:ι​(C)→C\theta\colon\iota(C)\to Cbyθ=σh​(φ0)−1​φ1\theta=\sigma_{h}(\varphi_{0})^{-1}\,\varphi_{1}. The equations
in (4.4) yieldθ=φ0​λC​ι​(φ1)−1=φ1​ι​(φ0)−1.\theta=\varphi_{0}\,\lambda_{C}\,\iota(\varphi_{1})^{-1}=\varphi_{1}\,\iota(\varphi_{0})^{-1}.

Therefore,θ​ι​(θ)=φ0​λC​ι​(φ1)−1​ι​(φ1)​ι​ι​(φ0)−1=λC\theta\,\iota(\theta)=\varphi_{0}\,\lambda_{C}\,\iota(\varphi_{1})^{-1}\,\iota(\varphi_{1})\,\iota\iota(\varphi_{0})^{-1}=\lambda_{C}. Moreover, the second
equation in (4.3) yieldsαC​ι​(h)=D​(λC​ι​(φ1)−1)​h​φ1=D​(φ0−1​θ)​h​φ1.\alpha_{C}\,\iota(h)=D(\lambda_{C}\,\iota(\varphi_{1})^{-1})\,h\,\varphi_{1}=D(\varphi_{0}^{-1}\,\theta)\,h\,\varphi_{1}.

But, by definition,θ=σh​(φ0)−1​φ1=h−1​D​(φ0)−1​h​φ1\theta=\sigma_{h}(\varphi_{0})^{-1}\,\varphi_{1}=h^{-1}\,D(\varphi_{0})^{-1}\,h\,\varphi_{1}, hence we
obtain by substituting in the last equationαC​ι​(h)=D​(θ)​h​θ.\alpha_{C}\,\iota(h)=D(\theta)\,h\,\theta.

This shows thatθ\thetais an isometry(ι​(C),αC​ι​(h))→(C,h)(\iota(C),\alpha_{C}\,\iota(h))\to(C,h)and completes the proof.
∎

Next, we show that the hypotheses on endomorphism algebras in
Proposition4.3pass on to the double category.

## Proposition 4.4.

LetC′=(C,f)∈𝒞′C^{\prime}=(C,f)\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}. IfEnd𝒞⁡(C)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)is semilocal and
rad-adically complete, thenEnd𝒞′⁡(C′)\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})also is semilocal
and rad-adically complete.

## Proof.

Letτ:End𝒞⁡(C)→End𝒞⁡(C)\tau\colon\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)be the automorphism
defined byτ​(φ)=f​ι​(φ)​f−1forφ∈End𝒞⁡(C).\tau(\varphi)=f\,\iota(\varphi)\,f^{-1}\qquad\text{for
$\varphi\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)$.}

It satisfiesτ2​(φ)=φ\tau^{2}(\varphi)=\varphifor allφ∈End𝒞⁡(C)\varphi\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)andEnd𝒞′⁡(C′)={φ∈End𝒞⁡(C)∣τ​(φ)=φ}.\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})=\{\varphi\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)\mid\tau(\varphi)=\varphi\}.

Therefore, the proposition follows from (b) in the next
ring-theoretic result:

## Lemma 4.5.

LetAAbe akk-algebra andτ:A→A\tau\colon A\to Aan automorphism
such thatτ2=IdA\tau^{2}=\operatorname{Id}_{A}, and setB={a∈A∣τ​(a)=a}B=\{a\in A\mid\tau(a)=a\}. WriteR​(A)R(A)(resp.R​(B)R(B)) for the Jacobson
radical ofAA(resp.BB).
- (a)

IfAAis right artinian andR​(A)=0R(A)=0, thenBBalso is right
artinian andR​(B)=0R(B)=0.
- (b)

IfAAis semilocal, thenR​(B)=B∩R​(A)R(B)=B\cap R(A)andBBis
semilocal. If moreoverAAis rad-adically complete, thenBBalso
is rad-adically complete.

## Proof.

(a)
SettingE={a∈A∣τ​(a)=−a}E=\{a\in A\mid\tau(a)=-a\}yields akk-module
decompositionA=B⊕EA=B\oplus E. Thekk-moduleEEis aBB-bimodule,
hence for every right idealI⊂BI\subset BI⋅A=I⊕I⋅EandI=(I⋅A)∩B.I\cdot A=I\oplus I\cdot E\qquad\text{and}\qquad I=(I\cdot A)\cap B.

If(In)n∈ℕ(I_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}is a descending chain of right ideals inBB, then there is an integerNNsuch thatIn⋅A=IN⋅AI_{n}\cdot A=I_{N}\cdot Afor alln≥Nn\geq N,
henceIn=(In⋅A)∩B=(IN⋅A)∩B=INfor alln≥N.I_{n}=(I_{n}\cdot A)\cap B=(I_{N}\cdot A)\cap B=I_{N}\quad\text{for all $n\geq N$.}

Thus,BBis right artinian.

## 4.2.Abelian categories

In this subsection, we assume that𝒞\mathcal{C}is an (essentially small)
abelian category, which we endow with its usual exact structure, and
we only consider dualities(D,δ)(D,\delta)withDDexact. For any
symmetric space(C,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)(C,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)and any subobjectφ:L↪C\varphi\colon L\hookrightarrow C, theorthogonal subobjectis
defined asL⟂:=ker⁡(D​(φ)​h)L^{\perp}:=\ker(D(\varphi)\,h). The space(C,h)(C,h)is
said to beisotropicif there is a nonzero subobjectL⊂CL\subset Csuch thatL⊂L⟂L\subset L^{\perp}, andanisotropicotherwise. (Note thatLLis not required to be a direct summand.) The
space(C,h)(C,h)is said to bemetabolic(weakly hyperbolicin[21, Def. 7.5.4]) if there is a subobjectL⊂CL\subset Csuch thatL=L⟂L=L^{\perp}. The resulting Witt groupW​(𝒞,D,δ)W(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)is
calledweak Witt groupin[21, Def. 7.5.4].

In every conjugation(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)we consider, the functorι\iotais assumed to be exact. The double category𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}is then
abelian, and the functorsFF,GG,Φ\Phi,Ψ\Psiare
exact. Therefore, given a conjugation(ι,λ)(\iota,\lambda)and a
commuting duality(D,δ)(D,\delta), the functors in the
octagon (3.2) induce Witt group homomorphisms, which
again yield the88-periodic diagram (4.1). Hyperbolic
spaces are clearly metabolic, hence Theorem3.1again
shows that this diagram is a chain complex. The goal of this
subsection is to show that the homology of this chain complex vanishes
when𝒞\mathcal{C}is an artinian category.

Recall that an abelian category𝒞\mathcal{C}is said to beartinian(resp.noetherian) if the descending (resp. ascending) chain
condition for
subobjects holds for everyC∈𝒞C\in\mathcal{C}. These properties are inherited
by the double category when𝒞\mathcal{C}carries a conjugation, since the
forgetful functorGGcarries subobjects to subobjects. If𝒞\mathcal{C}carries a duality, the artinian and the noetherian conditions are
equivalent, and imply that each object has finite length.

## Theorem 4.8.

If𝒞\mathcal{C}is an artinian abelian category, the
octagon of Witt groups (4.1) is exact.

## Proof.

By the same shifting argument
as in the proof of Theorem3.1, we are reduced to
proving the exactness of the sequenceW​(𝒞,D,δ)\textstyle{W(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}W​(Fwidehat)\scriptstyle{W(\widehat{F})}W​(𝒞′,D′,δ′)\textstyle{W(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}W​(Fwidehat′)\scriptstyle{W(\widehat{F}^{\prime})}W​(𝒞′′,D′′,δ′′).\textstyle{W(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}).}

We already know from Theorem3.1that this is a
zero-sequence. Let(C′,h′)∈𝒮​(𝒞′,D′,δ′)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})be a symmetric
space whose Witt equivalence class is in the kernel ofW​(Fwidehat′)W(\widehat{F}^{\prime}). Since𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}is artinian, isotropic reduction (also known as
sublagrangian reduction[2, §1.2.5]) yields an anisotropic
symmetric space Witt-equivalent to(C′,h′)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime}), see[22, Cor. 2.12]or[21, Th. 7.5.5]. We may therefore assume(C′,h′)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})is
anisotropic. Since the Witt class of(C′,h′)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})lies in the kernel ofW​(Fwidehat′)W(\widehat{F}^{\prime}), it follows from[26, Th. (4.9)]that𝒮​(Fwidehat′)​(C′,h′)\mathcal{S}(\widehat{F}^{\prime})(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})is metabolic. Lemma4.7shows that(C′,h′)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime})satisfies Property (P), hence
Lemma3.3yields a symmetric space(X,h)∈𝒮​(𝒞,D,δ)(X,h)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{C},D,\delta)whose Witt equivalence class is mapped
underW​(Fwidehat)W(\widehat{F})to the Witt equivalence class of(C′,h′)(C^{\prime},h^{\prime}).
∎

Theorem4.8shows that the corresponding octagon of Witt
groups is exact.
